People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

Well… I suppose what brings me in is—my family, mostly. My wife, the kids. I keep having this thought that they’d be better off if I just didn’t wake up one morning. I know that’s a terrible thing to say.

And I’ve been saying lately… I don’t need to see another winter. I don’t know. Winters used to be different, you know, when the house was busier and the furnace made that clanking noise all night… but now I just keep thinking maybe I’ve had enough.

  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It’s changed, yes. I used to look forward to bridge nights, and I had my garden out back—tomatoes never came in right unless I fussed over them—and the church cooking, too. My wife used to say I chopped onions too slow, but I liked being useful.

And Cubs games… I’d get excited about going, even if they lost, which they usually found a way to do. Now I mostly just sit in my chair. People mention those things and I know I ought to care, but I can’t seem to make myself.

My wife and the kids—well, they’ve had enough worry because of me, and I get to thinking they’d be better off without me here.\n\nAnd I’ve said it, too… that I don’t need to see another winter. I know that sounds awful. Winters used to be something, with the house busy and all that, but now it just feels like… maybe enough.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been having these thoughts about not waking up and feeling like your family would be better off without you?\n\nPatient: It started after my bowling partner died… that was, let me think, about a year and a month ago now. We used to go every week, same lane if we could get it—he had this way of fussing with the score even when it didn’t matter.\n\nAfter he was gone, that’s when those thoughts started creeping in. So… a little over a year, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself enjoying things you used to like, or has that changed for you over the past year?\n\nPatient: It’s changed, yes. I used to look forward to bridge nights, and I had my garden out back—tomatoes never came in right unless I fussed over them—and the church cooking, too. My wife used to say I chopped onions too slow, but I liked being useful.\n\nAnd Cubs games… I’d get excited about going, even if they lost, which they usually found a way to do. Now I mostly just sit in my chair. People mention those things and I know I ought to care, but I can’t seem to make myself.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure or enjoyment in?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I try to think of something… bridge, maybe, or the garden. I used to spend half a day with those tomato plants, and I’d complain about it, but I liked it. Church cooking, Cubs games too—those were things I looked forward to.\n\nNow I mostly just end up in my chair. Someone will say, “Come on, you used to enjoy this,” and I know they’re right, but it’s like I can’t reach the feeling anymore. I just can’t make myself care about any of it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

I try to think of something… bridge, maybe, or the garden. I used to spend half a day with those tomato plants, and I’d complain about it, but I liked it. Church cooking, Cubs games too—those were things I looked forward to.\n\nNow I mostly just end up in my chair. Someone will say, “Come on, you used to enjoy this,” and I know they’re right, but it’s like I can’t reach the feeling anymore. I just can’t make myself care about any of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight over the past year?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’ve kept track, honestly. My pants maybe feel a little different some days, but then laundry does that too—my wife used to say I’d blame the dryer for everything.\n\nI haven’t been weighing myself regular, so I couldn’t tell you if it’s a significant amount.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself moving or speaking more slowly than you used to?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I suppose I do. My wife says I move like I’m walking through deep snow. That’s how she puts it. I’ll be trying to get dressed and somehow it takes forever, like my arms don’t want to go where they’re supposed to.\n\nEven just crossing the kitchen… and it’s not a big kitchen, never was, though we did talk once about knocking out a wall—anyway, it takes me so long I sometimes just stop. I give up before I’ve done much of anything. Speaking too, maybe. I know I’m slower than I used to be.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty or blame yourself for things that have happened?\n\nPatient: Yes… all the time, really. I go back over things I said to him, things I didn’t say, the way I handled things with the family. Little things too, stupid little things from years ago, like forgetting someone’s birthday or snapping at the kids over nothing.\n\nI know people say you can’t fix the past, but my mind won’t leave it alone. It just keeps showing me every place I failed. And after a while it feels like… well, like I’m no good to anybody. Like whatever punishment is coming, I probably earned it.\n\nDoctor: Are there any small pleasures in life that you still look forward to or enjoy?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I used to have little things… bridge on Thursdays, if the fellows weren’t arguing too much about bidding, and the garden—tomatoes never came in right unless I fussed over them. Church cooking too, though I mostly chopped onions and got in the way. And Cubs games, of course, even when they were breaking your heart.\n\nBut now I just… sit in my chair. I think about doing those things, or someone mentions them, and I can’t make myself care. It’s like they belong to somebody else.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure or enjoyment in, no matter how small they may seem?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. I used to look forward to bridge, and fussing around in the garden, and the church cooking days—though half the time I was just peeling potatoes or chopping onions, you know, nothing important. And going to Cubs games, or even just watching them, that used to mean something.\n\nNow I mostly just sit in my chair. People ask if I want to do those things and I… I can’t seem to care. It all feels far away, like it belongs to someone else.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

No… not really. I keep trying to think of one, but nothing comes to mind.

I used to look forward to things, you know—bridge, puttering in the garden, helping with the church cooking. Even Cubs games, though they’d make a man suffer more than celebrate half the time. My brother used to say being a Cubs fan builds character, but I don’t know about that…

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I suppose what brings me in is—my family, mostly. My wife, the kids. I keep having this thought that they’d be better off if I just didn’t wake up one morning. I know that’s a terrible thing to say.\n\nAnd I’ve been saying lately… I don’t need to see another winter. I don’t know. Winters used to be different, you know, when the house was busier and the furnace made that clanking noise all night… but now I just keep thinking maybe I’ve had enough.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts of actually harming yourself or making an attempt to end your life?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I don’t think of it like making some big attempt, if that’s what you mean. It’s more that I keep thinking maybe if I just didn’t wake up, that would be all right. My wife and the kids—well, they’ve had enough worry because of me, and I get to thinking they’d be better off without me here.\n\nAnd I’ve said it, too… that I don’t need to see another winter. I know that sounds awful. Winters used to be something, with the house busy and all that, but now it just feels like… maybe enough.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been having these thoughts about not waking up and feeling like your family would be better off without you?\n\nPatient: It started after my bowling partner died… that was, let me think, about a year and a month ago now. We used to go every week, same lane if we could get it—he had this way of fussing with the score even when it didn’t matter.\n\nAfter he was gone, that’s when those thoughts started creeping in. So… a little over a year, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself enjoying things you used to like, or has that changed for you over the past year?\n\nPatient: It’s changed, yes. I used to look forward to bridge nights, and I had my garden out back—tomatoes never came in right unless I fussed over them—and the church cooking, too. My wife used to say I chopped onions too slow, but I liked being useful.\n\nAnd Cubs games… I’d get excited about going, even if they lost, which they usually found a way to do. Now I mostly just sit in my chair. People mention those things and I know I ought to care, but I can’t seem to make myself.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure or enjoyment in?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I try to think of something… bridge, maybe, or the garden. I used to spend half a day with those tomato plants, and I’d complain about it, but I liked it. Someone will say, “Come on, you used to enjoy this,” and I know they’re right, but it’s like I can’t reach the feeling anymore. I just can’t make myself care about any of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight over the past year?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’ve kept track, honestly. My wife says I move like I’m walking through deep snow. That’s how she puts it. It’s more that I don’t care anymore, I think. I mean, I could probably still sit at a bridge table if someone dragged me there… though I’d forget half the bidding, I suppose. My wife used to say I always overbid anyway.

But no, it’s not just that I can’t do them. The garden’s still there, the church still does the cooking days, and the Cubs are still breaking hearts like they always do. I used to look forward to all that. Now I just sit in my chair and… I can’t make myself care about any of it.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.1, F33.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I suppose what brings me in is—my family, mostly. My wife, the kids. I keep having this thought that they’d be better off if I just didn’t wake up one morning. I know that’s a terrible thing to say.\n\nAnd I’ve been saying lately… I don’t need to see another winter. I don’t know. Winters used to be different, you know, when the house was busier and the furnace made that clanking noise all night… but now I just keep thinking maybe I’ve had enough.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts of actually harming yourself or making an attempt to end your life?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I don’t think of it like making some big attempt, if that’s what you mean. It’s more that I keep thinking maybe if I just didn’t wake up, that would be all right. My wife and the kids—well, they’ve had enough worry because of me, and I get to thinking they’d be better off without me here.\n\nAnd I’ve said it, too… that I don’t need to see another winter. I know that sounds awful. Winters used to be something, with the house busy and all that, but now it just feels like… maybe enough.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been having these thoughts about not waking up and feeling like your family would be better off without you?\n\nPatient: It started after my bowling partner died… that was, let me think, about a year and a month ago now. We used to go every week, same lane if we could get it—he had this way of fussing with the score even when it didn’t matter.\n\nAfter he was gone, that’s when those thoughts started creeping in. So… a little over a year, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself enjoying things you used to like, or has that changed for you over the past year?\n\nPatient: It’s changed, yes. I used to look forward to bridge nights, and I had my garden out back—tomatoes never came in right unless I fussed over them—and the church cooking, too. My wife used to say I chopped onions too slow, but I liked being useful.\n\nAnd Cubs games… I’d get excited about going, even if they lost, which they usually found a way to do. Now I mostly just sit in my chair. People mention those things and I know I ought to care, but I can’t seem to make myself.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure or enjoyment in?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I try to think of something… bridge, maybe, or the garden. I used to spend half a day with those tomato plants, and I’d complain about it, but I liked it. Church cooking, Cubs games too—those were things I looked forward to.\n\nNow I mostly just end up in my chair. Someone will say, “Come on, you used to enjoy this,” and I know they’re right, but it’s like I can’t reach the feeling anymore. I just can’t make myself care about any of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight over the past year?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’ve kept track, honestly. My pants maybe feel a little different some days, but then laundry does that too—my wife used to say I’d blame the dryer for everything.\n\nI haven’t been weighing myself regular, so I couldn’t tell you if it’s a significant amount.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself moving or speaking more slowly than you used to?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I suppose I do. My wife says I move like I’m walking through deep snow. That’s how she puts it. I’ll be trying to get dressed and somehow it takes forever, like my arms don’t want to go where they’re supposed to.\n\nEven just crossing the kitchen… and it’s not a big kitchen, never was, though we did talk once about knocking out a wall—anyway, it takes me so long I sometimes just stop. I give up before I’ve done much of anything. Speaking too, maybe. I know I’m slower than I used to be.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty or blame yourself for things that have happened?\n\nPatient: Yes… all the time, really. I go back over things I said to him, things I didn’t say, the way I handled things with the family. Little things too, stupid little things from years ago, like forgetting someone’s birthday or snapping at the kids over nothing.\n\nI know people say you can’t fix the past, but my mind won’t leave it alone. It just keeps showing me every place I failed. And after a while it feels like… well, like I’m no good to anybody. Like whatever punishment is coming, I probably earned it.\n\nDoctor: Are there any small pleasures in life that you still look forward to or enjoy?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I used to have little things… bridge on Thursdays, if the fellows weren’t arguing too much about bidding, and the garden—tomatoes never came in right unless I fussed over them. Church cooking too, though I mostly chopped onions and got in the way. And Cubs games, of course, even when they were breaking your heart.\n\nBut now I just… sit in my chair. I think about doing those things, or someone mentions them, and I can’t make myself care. It’s like they belong to somebody else.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure or enjoyment in, no matter how small they may seem?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. I used to look forward to bridge, and fussing around in the garden, and the church cooking days—though half the time I was just peeling potatoes or chopping onions, you know, nothing important. And going to Cubs games, or even just watching them, that used to mean something.\n\nNow I mostly just sit in my chair. People ask if I want to do those things and I… I can’t seem to care. It all feels far away, like it belongs to someone else.\n\nDoctor: Are there any small things that you still find give you some happiness or pleasure?\n\nPatient: No… not really. I keep trying to think of one, but nothing comes to mind.\n\nI used to look forward to things, you know—bridge, puttering in the garden, helping with the church cooking. Even Cubs games, though they’d make a man suffer more than celebrate half the time. My brother used to say being a Cubs fan builds character, but I don’t know about that…\n\nAnyway, now I mostly just sit in my chair. People mention those things and I know I used to care, but I can’t seem to feel anything about them now.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way, with little interest in activities and thoughts about not waking up?\n\nPatient: It’s been… since my bowling partner died. That’s when I started feeling this way, more or less. A little over a year.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in activities that you used to enjoy, or is it more that you're just not able to do them like you used to?\n\nPatient: It’s more that I don’t care anymore, I think. You are about\nto talk with the doctor.\n\nLooking at the dialogue so far and the doctor's last question, work out what it is asking\nabout, and pick the item of your symptom profile to answer it with.\n\n[Symptom Profiles]\n- key: S019\n  name: Delusions\n  description: Fixed beliefs that do not change easily, even in the face of contradictory evidence.\n  manifestation: I know it sounds foolish to everyone else, but I’m certain I’m being punished for letting my bowling partner down, and no amount of my wife or pastor telling me otherwise can shake it.\n- key: S020\n  name: Hallucinations\n  description: Perception-like experiences that occur without an external stimulus and are as vivid and clear as normal perception.\n  manifestation: Some evenings I hear his voice plain as day from the hallway or the spare room, telling me I should have been there or that I failed him, even when my wife says the house is quiet.\n- key: S025\n  name: Loss_of_Interest\n  description: A state where interest or pleasure in previously enjoyed activities is markedly diminished.\n  manifestation: I used to look forward to bridge, the garden, church cooking, and going to Cubs games, but now I just sit in my chair and can’t make myself care about any of it.\n- key: S028\n  name: Psychomotor_Changes\n  description: Observable increase or decrease in psychomotor activity.\n  manifestation: My wife says I move like I’m walking through deep snow, and even getting dressed or crossing the kitchen takes me so long that I give up before I’ve done much of anything.\n- key: S030\n  name: Worthlessness_Guilt\n  description: Feelings of worthlessness or excessive or inappropriate guilt (which may be delusional; I keep trying to think of one, but nothing comes to mind.\n\nI used to look forward to things, you know—bridge, puttering in the garden, helping with the church cooking. Even Cubs games, though they’d make a man suffer more than celebrate half the time. People mention those things and I know I used to care, but I can’t seem to feel anything about them now.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way, with little interest in activities and thoughts about not waking up?\n\nPatient: It’s been… since my bowling partner died. That was about a year and a month ago now, I think.\n\nFunny, we weren’t even the best bowlers in the league—he had this way of blaming the lanes every time he missed a spare—but after he was gone, things just… went flat. That’s when I started feeling this way, more or less. A little over a year.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in activities that you used to enjoy, or is it more that you're just not able to do them like you used to?\n\nPatient: It’s more that I don’t care anymore, I think. I mean, I could probably still sit at a bridge table if someone dragged me there… though I’d forget half the bidding, I suppose. My wife used to say I always overbid anyway.\n\nBut no, it’s not just that I can’t do them. The garden’s still there, the church still does the cooking days, and the Cubs are still breaking hearts like they always do. I used to look forward to all that. No… I don’t think it really went away. It’s been pretty steady since my bowling partner died—about a year and a month now.

We used to bowl on Thursdays, and he’d always complain about the oil on the lanes like it was a conspiracy… but after he passed, it just all sort of settled over me. I can’t really point to a stretch where I felt like myself again and then it came back. It’s just been there.

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n14\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.1, F33.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I suppose what brings me in is—my family, mostly. My wife, the kids. I keep having this thought that they’d be better off if I just didn’t wake up one morning. I know that’s a terrible thing to say.\n\nAnd I’ve been saying lately… I don’t need to see another winter. I don’t know. Winters used to be different, you know, when the house was busier and the furnace made that clanking noise all night… but now I just keep thinking maybe I’ve had enough.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts of actually harming yourself or making an attempt to end your life?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I don’t think of it like making some big attempt, if that’s what you mean. It’s more that I keep thinking maybe if I just didn’t wake up, that would be all right. My wife and the kids—well, they’ve had enough worry because of me, and I get to thinking they’d be better off without me here.\n\nAnd I’ve said it, too… that I don’t need to see another winter. I know that sounds awful. Winters used to be something, with the house busy and all that, but now it just feels like… maybe enough.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been having these thoughts about not waking up and feeling like your family would be better off without you?\n\nPatient: It started after my bowling partner died… that was, let me think, about a year and a month ago now. We used to go every week, same lane if we could get it—he had this way of fussing with the score even when it didn’t matter.\n\nAfter he was gone, that’s when those thoughts started creeping in. So… a little over a year, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself enjoying things you used to like, or has that changed for you over the past year?\n\nPatient: It’s changed, yes. I used to look forward to bridge nights, and I had my garden out back—tomatoes never came in right unless I fussed over them—and the church cooking, too. My wife used to say I chopped onions too slow, but I liked being useful.\n\nAnd Cubs games… I’d get excited about going, even if they lost, which they usually found a way to do. Now I mostly just sit in my chair. People mention those things and I know I ought to care, but I can’t seem to make myself.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure or enjoyment in?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I try to think of something… bridge, maybe, or the garden. I used to spend half a day with those tomato plants, and I’d complain about it, but I liked it. Church cooking, Cubs games too—those were things I looked forward to.\n\nNow I mostly just end up in my chair. Someone will say, “Come on, you used to enjoy this,” and I know they’re right, but it’s like I can’t reach the feeling anymore. I just can’t make myself care about any of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight over the past year?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’ve kept track, honestly. My pants maybe feel a little different some days, but then laundry does that too—my wife used to say I’d blame the dryer for everything.\n\nI haven’t been weighing myself regular, so I couldn’t tell you if it’s a significant amount.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself moving or speaking more slowly than you used to?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I suppose I do. My wife says I move like I’m walking through deep snow. That’s how she puts it. I’ll be trying to get dressed and somehow it takes forever, like my arms don’t want to go where they’re supposed to.\n\nEven just crossing the kitchen… and it’s not a big kitchen, never was, though we did talk once about knocking out a wall—anyway, it takes me so long I sometimes just stop. I give up before I’ve done much of anything. Speaking too, maybe. I know I’m slower than I used to be.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty or blame yourself for things that have happened?\n\nPatient: Yes… all the time, really. I go back over things I said to him, things I didn’t say, the way I handled things with the family. Little things too, stupid little things from years ago, like forgetting someone’s birthday or snapping at the kids over nothing.\n\nI know people say you can’t fix the past, but my mind won’t leave it alone. It just keeps showing me every place I failed. And after a while it feels like… well, like I’m no good to anybody. Like whatever punishment is coming, I probably earned it.\n\nDoctor: Are there any small pleasures in life that you still look forward to or enjoy?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I used to have little things… bridge on Thursdays, if the fellows weren’t arguing too much about bidding, and the garden—tomatoes never came in right unless I fussed over them. Church cooking too, though I mostly chopped onions and got in the way. And Cubs games, of course, even when they were breaking your heart.\n\nBut now I just… sit in my chair. I think about doing those things, or someone mentions them, and I can’t make myself care. It’s like they belong to somebody else.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure or enjoyment in, no matter how small they may seem?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. I used to look forward to bridge, and fussing around in the garden, and the church cooking days—though half the time I was just peeling potatoes or chopping onions, you know, nothing important. And going to Cubs games, or even just watching them, that used to mean something.\n\nNow I mostly just sit in my chair. People ask if I want to do those things and I… I can’t seem to care. It all feels far away, like it belongs to someone else.\n\nDoctor: Are there any small things that you still find give you some happiness or pleasure?\n\nPatient: No… not really. I keep trying to think of one, but nothing comes to mind.\n\nI used to look forward to things, you know—bridge, puttering in the garden, helping with the church cooking. Even Cubs games, though they’d make a man suffer more than celebrate half the time. My brother used to say being a Cubs fan builds character, but I don’t know about that…\n\nAnyway, now I mostly just sit in my chair. People mention those things and I know I used to care, but I can’t seem to feel anything about them now.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way, with little interest in activities and thoughts about not waking up?\n\nPatient: It’s been… since my bowling partner died. That was about a year and a month ago now, I think.\n\nFunny, we weren’t even the best bowlers in the league—he had this way of blaming the lanes every time he missed a spare—but after he was gone, things just… went flat. That’s when I started feeling this way, more or less. A little over a year.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in activities that you used to enjoy, or is it more that you're just not able to do them like you used to?\n\nPatient: It’s more that I don’t care anymore, I think. I mean, I could probably still sit at a bridge table if someone dragged me there… though I’d forget half the bidding, I suppose. My wife used to say I always overbid anyway.\n\nBut no, it’s not just that I can’t do them. The garden’s still there, the church still does the cooking days, and the Cubs are still breaking hearts like they always do. I used to look forward to all that. Now I just sit in my chair and… I can’t make myself care about any of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you had periods where these feelings of not caring and thoughts of not waking up went away, only to come back?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think it really went away. It’s been pretty steady since my bowling partner died—about a year and a month now.\n\nWe used to bowl on Thursdays, and he’d always complain about the oil on the lanes like it was a conspiracy… but after he passed, it just all sort of settled over me. I can’t really point to a stretch where I felt like myself again and then it came back. It’s just been there.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I suppose what brings me in is—my family, mostly. My wife, the kids. I keep having this thought that they’d be better off if I just didn’t wake up one morning. I know that’s a terrible thing to say.\n\nAnd I’ve been saying lately… I don’t need to see another winter. I don’t know. Winters used to be different, you know, when the house was busier and the furnace made that clanking noise all night… but now I just keep thinking maybe I’ve had enough.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts of actually harming yourself or making an attempt to end your life?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I don’t think of it like making some big attempt, if that’s what you mean. It’s more that I keep thinking maybe if I just didn’t wake up, that would be all right. My wife and the kids—well, they’ve had enough worry because of me, and I get to thinking they’d be better off without me here.\n\nAnd I’ve said it, too… that I don’t need to see another winter. I know that sounds awful. Winters used to be something, with the house busy and all that, but now it just feels like… maybe enough.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been having these thoughts about not waking up and feeling like your family would be better off without you?\n\nPatient: It started after my bowling partner died… that was, let me think, about a year and a month ago now. We used to go every week, same lane if we could get it—he had this way of fussing with the score even when it didn’t matter.\n\nAfter he was gone, that’s when those thoughts started creeping in. So… a little over a year, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself enjoying things you used to like, or has that changed for you over the past year?\n\nPatient: It’s changed, yes. I used to look forward to bridge nights, and I had my garden out back—tomatoes never came in right unless I fussed over them—and the church cooking, too. My wife used to say I chopped onions too slow, but I liked being useful.\n\nAnd Cubs games… I’d get excited about going, even if they lost, which they usually found a way to do. Now I mostly just sit in my chair. People mention those things and I know I ought to care, but I can’t seem to make myself.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure or enjoyment in?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I try to think of something… bridge, maybe, or the garden. I used to spend half a day with those tomato plants, and I’d complain about it, but I liked it. Church cooking, Cubs games too—those were things I looked forward to.\n\nNow I mostly just end up in my chair. Someone will say, “Come on, you used to enjoy this,” and I know they’re right, but it’s like I can’t reach the feeling anymore. I just can’t make myself care about any of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight over the past year?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’ve kept track, honestly. My pants maybe feel a little different some days, but then laundry does that too—my wife used to say I’d blame the dryer for everything.\n\nI haven’t been weighing myself regular, so I couldn’t tell you if it’s a significant amount.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself moving or speaking more slowly than you used to?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I suppose I do. My wife says I move like I’m walking through deep snow. That’s how she puts it. I’ll be trying to get dressed and somehow it takes forever, like my arms don’t want to go where they’re supposed to.\n\nEven just crossing the kitchen… and it’s not a big kitchen, never was, though we did talk once about knocking out a wall—anyway, it takes me so long I sometimes just stop. I give up before I’ve done much of anything. Speaking too, maybe. I know I’m slower than I used to be.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty or blame yourself for things that have happened?\n\nPatient: Yes… all the time, really. I go back over things I said to him, things I didn’t say, the way I handled things with the family. It just keeps showing me every place I failed. And after a while it feels like… well, like I’m no good to anybody. Like whatever punishment is coming, I probably earned it.\n\nDoctor: Are there any small pleasures in life that you still look forward to or enjoy?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. Church cooking too, though I mostly chopped onions and got in the way. And Cubs games, of course, even when they were breaking your heart.\n\nBut now I just… sit in my chair. I think about doing those things, or someone mentions them, and I can’t make myself care. It’s like they belong to somebody else.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure or enjoyment in, no matter how small they may seem?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. I used to look forward to bridge, and fussing around in the garden, and the church cooking days—though half the time I was just peeling potatoes or chopping onions, you know, nothing important. And going to Cubs games, or even just watching them, that used to mean something.\n\nNow I mostly just sit in my chair. People ask if I want to do those things and I… I can’t seem to care. It all feels far away, like it belongs to someone else.\n\nDoctor: Are there any small things that you still find give you some happiness or pleasure?\n\nPatient: No… not really. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.1, F33.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I suppose what brings me in is—my family, mostly. My wife, the kids. I keep having this thought that they’d be better off if I just didn’t wake up one morning. I know that’s a terrible thing to say.\n\nAnd I’ve been saying lately… I don’t need to see another winter. I don’t know. Winters used to be different, you know, when the house was busier and the furnace made that clanking noise all night… but now I just keep thinking maybe I’ve had enough.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts of actually harming yourself or making an attempt to end your life?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I don’t think of it like making some big attempt, if that’s what you mean. It’s more that I keep thinking maybe if I just didn’t wake up, that would be all right. My wife and the kids—well, they’ve had enough worry because of me, and I get to thinking they’d be better off without me here.\n\nAnd I’ve said it, too… that I don’t need to see another winter. I know that sounds awful. Winters used to be something, with the house busy and all that, but now it just feels like… maybe enough.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been having these thoughts about not waking up and feeling like your family would be better off without you?\n\nPatient: It started after my bowling partner died… that was, let me think, about a year and a month ago now. We used to go every week, same lane if we could get it—he had this way of fussing with the score even when it didn’t matter.\n\nAfter he was gone, that’s when those thoughts started creeping in. So… a little over a year, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself enjoying things you used to like, or has that changed for you over the past year?\n\nPatient: It’s changed, yes. I used to look forward to bridge nights, and I had my garden out back—tomatoes never came in right unless I fussed over them—and the church cooking, too. My wife used to say I chopped onions too slow, but I liked being useful.\n\nAnd Cubs games… I’d get excited about going, even if they lost, which they usually found a way to do. Now I mostly just sit in my chair. People mention those things and I know I ought to care, but I can’t seem to make myself.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure or enjoyment in?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I try to think of something… bridge, maybe, or the garden. I used to spend half a day with those tomato plants, and I’d complain about it, but I liked it. Church cooking, Cubs games too—those were things I looked forward to.\n\nNow I mostly just end up in my chair. Someone will say, “Come on, you used to enjoy this,” and I know they’re right, but it’s like I can’t reach the feeling anymore. I just can’t make myself care about any of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight over the past year?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’ve kept track, honestly. My pants maybe feel a little different some days, but then laundry does that too—my wife used to say I’d blame the dryer for everything.\n\nI haven’t been weighing myself regular, so I couldn’t tell you if it’s a significant amount.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself moving or speaking more slowly than you used to?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I suppose I do. My wife says I move like I’m walking through deep snow. That’s how she puts it. I’ll be trying to get dressed and somehow it takes forever, like my arms don’t want to go where they’re supposed to.\n\nEven just crossing the kitchen… and it’s not a big kitchen, never was, though we did talk once about knocking out a wall—anyway, it takes me so long I sometimes just stop. I give up before I’ve done much of anything. Speaking too, maybe. I know I’m slower than I used to be.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty or blame yourself for things that have happened?\n\nPatient: Yes… all the time, really. I go back over things I said to him, things I didn’t say, the way I handled things with the family. Little things too, stupid little things from years ago, like forgetting someone’s birthday or snapping at the kids over nothing.\n\nI know people say you can’t fix the past, but my mind won’t leave it alone. It just keeps showing me every place I failed. And after a while it feels like… well, like I’m no good to anybody. Like whatever punishment is coming, I probably earned it.\n\nDoctor: Are there any small pleasures in life that you still look forward to or enjoy?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I used to have little things… bridge on Thursdays, if the fellows weren’t arguing too much about bidding, and the garden—tomatoes never came in right unless I fussed over them. Church cooking too, though I mostly chopped onions and got in the way. And Cubs games, of course, even when they were breaking your heart.\n\nBut now I just… sit in my chair. I think about doing those things, or someone mentions them, and I can’t make myself care. It’s like they belong to somebody else.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure or enjoyment in, no matter how small they may seem?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. I used to look forward to bridge, and fussing around in the garden, and the church cooking days—though half the time I was just peeling potatoes or chopping onions, you know, nothing important. And going to Cubs games, or even just watching them, that used to mean something.\n\nNow I mostly just sit in my chair. People ask if I want to do those things and I… I can’t seem to care. It all feels far away, like it belongs to someone else.\n\nDoctor: Are there any small things that you still find give you some happiness or pleasure?\n\nPatient: No… not really. I keep trying to think of one, but nothing comes to mind.\n\nI used to look forward to things, you know—bridge, puttering in the garden, helping with the church cooking. Even Cubs games, though they’d make a man suffer more than celebrate half the time. My brother used to say being a Cubs fan builds character, but I don’t know about that…\n\nAnyway, now I mostly just sit in my chair. People mention those things and I know I used to care, but I can’t seem to feel anything about them now.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way, with little interest in activities and thoughts about not waking up?\n\nPatient: It’s been… since my bowling partner died. That was about a year and a month ago now, I think.\n\nFunny, we weren’t even the best bowlers in the league—he had this way of blaming the lanes every time he missed a spare—but after he was gone, things just… went flat. That’s when I started feeling this way, more or less. A little over a year.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in activities that you used to enjoy, or is it more that you're just not able to do them like you used to?\n\nPatient: It’s more that I don’t care anymore, I think. I mean, I could probably still sit at a bridge table if someone dragged me there… though I’d forget half the bidding, I suppose. My wife used to say I always overbid anyway.\n\nBut no, it’s not just that I can’t do them. Now I just sit in my chair and… I can’t make myself care about any of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you had periods where these feelings of not caring and thoughts of not waking up went away, only to come back?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think it really went away. It’s been pretty steady since my bowling partner died—about a year and a month now.\n\nWe used to bowl on Thursdays, and he’d always complain about the oil on the lanes like it was a conspiracy… but after he passed, it just all sort of settled over me. Church cooking too, though I mostly chopped onions and got in the way. And Cubs games, of course, even when they were breaking your heart.\n\nBut now I just… sit in my chair. I think about doing those things, or someone mentions them, and I can’t make myself care. It’s like they belong to somebody else.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure or enjoyment in, no matter how small they may seem?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. I used to look forward to bridge, and fussing around in the garden, and the church cooking days—though half the time I was just peeling potatoes or chopping onions, you know, nothing important. And going to Cubs games, or even just watching them, that used to mean something.\n\nNow I mostly just sit in my chair. People ask if I want to do those things and I… I can’t seem to care. It all feels far away, like it belongs to someone else.\n\nDoctor: Are there any small things that you still find give you some happiness or pleasure?\n\nPatient: No… not really. I keep trying to think of one, but nothing comes to mind.\n\nI used to look forward to things, you know—bridge, puttering in the garden, helping with the church cooking. Even Cubs games, though they’d make a man suffer more than celebrate half the time. My brother used to say being a Cubs fan builds character, but I don’t know about that…\n\nAnyway, now I mostly just sit in my chair. People mention those things and I know I used to care, but I can’t seem to feel anything about them now.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way, with little interest in activities and thoughts about not waking up?\n\nPatient: It’s been… since my bowling partner died. That was about a year and a month ago now, I think.\n\nFunny, we weren’t even the best bowlers in the league—he had this way of blaming the lanes every time he missed a spare—but after he was gone, things just… went flat. That’s when I started feeling this way, more or less. A little over a year.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in activities that you used to enjoy, or is it more that you're just not able to do them like you used to?\n\nPatient: It’s more that I don’t care anymore, I think. I mean, I could probably still sit at a bridge table if someone dragged me there… though I’d forget half the bidding, I suppose. My wife used to say I always overbid anyway.\n\nBut no, it’s not just that I can’t do them. The garden’s still there, the church still does the cooking days, and the Cubs are still breaking hearts like they always do. I used to look forward to all that. Now I just sit in my chair and… I can’t make myself care about any of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you had periods where these feelings of not caring and thoughts of not waking up went away, only to come back?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think it really went away. It’s been pretty steady since my bowling partner died—about a year and a month now.\n\nWe used to bowl on Thursdays, and he’d always complain about the oil on the lanes like it was a conspiracy… but after he passed, it just all sort of settled over me. I can’t really point to a stretch where I felt like myself again and then it came back. It’s just been there.\n\nDoctor: Are these feelings of not caring and thoughts of not waking up something you experience for most of the day?\n\nPatient: Most of the day, yes… I’d say so. I mean, I sit in that chair and the day just sort of goes by.\n\nI used to look forward to things—bridge, puttering in the garden, helping with the church cooking, even going to Cubs games, though Lord knows they test a man’s patience. Now it’s like… they’re all still there, but I can’t make myself care enough to get up for them. It’s just there with me most of the time.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling this way, with little interest in things and thoughts about not waking up, for most of every day over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yes… most every day, I suppose. Not just the last two weeks. It’s been going on since my bowling partner died—about a year and a month now.\n\nFunny thing, he used to keep score even when the machine did it, said he didn’t trust computers… but after he was gone, everything just kind of… stayed gray. So yes, over the past two weeks too.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Winters used to be something, with the house busy and all that, but now it just feels like… maybe enough.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been having these thoughts about not waking up and feeling like your family would be better off without you?\n\nPatient: It started after my bowling partner died… that was, let me think, about a year and a month ago now. We used to go every week, same lane if we could get it—he had this way of fussing with the score even when it didn’t matter.\n\nAfter he was gone, that’s when those thoughts started creeping in. So… a little over a year, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself enjoying things you used to like, or has that changed for you over the past year?\n\nPatient: It’s changed, yes. I used to look forward to bridge nights, and I had my garden out back—tomatoes never came in right unless I fussed over them—and the church cooking, too. My wife used to say I chopped onions too slow, but I liked being useful.\n\nAnd Cubs games… I’d get excited about going, even if they lost, which they usually found a way to do. Now I mostly just sit in my chair. People mention those things and I know I ought to care, but I can’t seem to make myself.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure or enjoyment in?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I try to think of something… bridge, maybe, or the garden. I used to spend half a day with those tomato plants, and I’d complain about it, but I liked it. Church cooking, Cubs games too—those were things I looked forward to.\n\nNow I mostly just end up in my chair. Someone will say, “Come on, you used to enjoy this,” and I know they’re right, but it’s like I can’t reach the feeling anymore. I just can’t make myself care about any of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight over the past year?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’ve kept track, honestly. My pants maybe feel a little different some days, but then laundry does that too—my wife used to say I’d blame the dryer for everything.\n\nI haven’t been weighing myself regular, so I couldn’t tell you if it’s a significant amount.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself moving or speaking more slowly than you used to?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I suppose I do. My wife says I move like I’m walking through deep snow. That’s how she puts it. I’ll be trying to get dressed and somehow it takes forever, like my arms don’t want to go where they’re supposed to.\n\nEven just crossing the kitchen… and it’s not a big kitchen, never was, though we did talk once about knocking out a wall—anyway, it takes me so long I sometimes just stop. I give up before I’ve done much of anything. Speaking too, maybe. I know I’m slower than I used to be.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty or blame yourself for things that have happened?\n\nPatient: Yes… all the time, really. I go back over things I said to him, things I didn’t say, the way I handled things with the family. Little things too, stupid little things from years ago, like forgetting someone’s birthday or snapping at the kids over nothing.\n\nI know people say you can’t fix the past, but my mind won’t leave it alone. It just keeps showing me every place I failed. And after a while it feels like… well, like I’m no good to anybody. Like whatever punishment is coming, I probably earned it.\n\nDoctor: Are there any small pleasures in life that you still look forward to or enjoy?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I used to have little things… bridge on Thursdays, if the fellows weren’t arguing too much about bidding, and the garden—tomatoes never came in right unless I fussed over them. Church cooking too, though I mostly chopped onions and got in the way. And Cubs games, of course, even when they were breaking your heart.\n\nBut now I just… sit in my chair. I think about doing those things, or someone mentions them, and I can’t make myself care. It’s like they belong to somebody else.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure or enjoyment in, no matter how small they may seem?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. I used to look forward to bridge, and fussing around in the garden, and the church cooking days—though half the time I was just peeling potatoes or chopping onions, you know, nothing important. And going to Cubs games, or even just watching them, that used to mean something.\n\nNow I mostly just sit in my chair. People ask if I want to do those things and I… I can’t seem to care. It all feels far away, like it belongs to someone else.\n\nDoctor: Are there any small things that you still find give you some happiness or pleasure?\n\nPatient: No… not really. I keep trying to think of one, but nothing comes to mind.\n\nI used to look forward to things, you know—bridge, puttering in the garden, helping with the church cooking. Even Cubs games, though they’d make a man suffer more than celebrate half the time. My brother used to say being a Cubs fan builds character, but I don’t know about that…\n\nAnyway, now I mostly just sit in my chair. People mention those things and I know I used to care, but I can’t seem to feel anything about them now.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way, with little interest in activities and thoughts about not waking up?\n\nPatient: It’s been… since my bowling partner died. That was about a year and a month ago now, I think.\n\nFunny, we weren’t even the best bowlers in the league—he had this way of blaming the lanes every time he missed a spare—but after he was gone, things just… went flat. That’s when I started feeling this way, more or less. A little over a year.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in activities that you used to enjoy, or is it more that you're just not able to do them like you used to?\n\nPatient: It’s more that I don’t care anymore, I think. I mean, I could probably still sit at a bridge table if someone dragged me there… though I’d forget half the bidding, I suppose. My wife used to say I always overbid anyway.\n\nBut no, it’s not just that I can’t do them. The garden’s still there, the church still does the cooking days, and the Cubs are still breaking hearts like they always do. I used to look forward to all that. Now I just sit in my chair and… I can’t make myself care about any of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you had periods where these feelings of not caring and thoughts of not waking up went away, only to come back?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think it really went away. It’s been pretty steady since my bowling partner died—about a year and a month now.\n\nWe used to bowl on Thursdays, and he’d always complain about the oil on the lanes like it was a conspiracy… but after he passed, it just all sort of settled over me. I can’t really point to a stretch where I felt like myself again and then it came back. It’s just been there.\n\nDoctor: Are these feelings of not caring and thoughts of not waking up something you experience for most of the day?\n\nPatient: Most of the day, yes… I’d say so. I mean, I sit in that chair and the day just sort of goes by.\n\nI used to look forward to things—bridge, puttering in the garden, helping with the church cooking, even going to Cubs games, though Lord knows they test a man’s patience. Now it’s like… they’re all still there, but I can’t make myself care enough to get up for them. It’s just there with me most of the time.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling this way, with little interest in things and thoughts about not waking up, for most of every day over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yes… most every day, I suppose. Not just the last two weeks. It’s been going on since my bowling partner died—about a year and a month now.\n\nFunny thing, he used to keep score even when the machine did it, said he didn’t trust computers… but after he was gone, everything just kind of… stayed gray. So yes, over the past two weeks too.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you've stopped doing because you no longer find pleasure in them?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I’ve stopped most of it, I suppose. Bridge, for one. I used to like sitting there with the cards, trying to remember who bid what—my wife used to say I played too slow, but then she said that about how I drove too.\n\nAnd the garden… I used to fuss over the tomatoes like they were children. Church cooking too, and going to Cubs games. I used to look forward to all that. Now I mostly just sit in my chair, and I can’t make myself care enough to go. It’s all still there, but it doesn’t feel like anything to me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I’ll be trying to get dressed and somehow it takes forever, like my arms don’t want to go where they’re supposed to.\n\nEven just crossing the kitchen… and it’s not a big kitchen, never was, though we did talk once about knocking out a wall—anyway, it takes me so long I sometimes just stop. I give up before I’ve done much of anything. Speaking too, maybe. I know I’m slower than I used to be.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty or blame yourself for things that have happened?\n\nPatient: Yes… all the time, really. I go back over things I said to him, things I didn’t say, the way I handled things with the family. Little things too, stupid little things from years ago, like forgetting someone’s birthday or snapping at the kids over nothing.\n\nI know people say you can’t fix the past, but my mind won’t leave it alone. It just keeps showing me every place I failed. And after a while it feels like… well, like I’m no good to anybody. Like whatever punishment is coming, I probably earned it.\n\nDoctor: Are there any small pleasures in life that you still look forward to or enjoy?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I used to have little things… bridge on Thursdays, if the fellows weren’t arguing too much about bidding, and the garden—tomatoes never came in right unless I fussed over them. Church cooking too, though I mostly chopped onions and got in the way. And Cubs games, of course, even when they were breaking your heart.\n\nBut now I just… sit in my chair. I think about doing those things, or someone mentions them, and I can’t make myself care. It’s like they belong to somebody else.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure or enjoyment in, no matter how small they may seem?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. I used to look forward to bridge, and fussing around in the garden, and the church cooking days—though half the time I was just peeling potatoes or chopping onions, you know, nothing important. And going to Cubs games, or even just watching them, that used to mean something.\n\nNow I mostly just sit in my chair. People ask if I want to do those things and I… I can’t seem to care. It all feels far away, like it belongs to someone else.\n\nDoctor: Are there any small things that you still find give you some happiness or pleasure?\n\nPatient: No… not really. I keep trying to think of one, but nothing comes to mind.\n\nI used to look forward to things, you know—bridge, puttering in the garden, helping with the church cooking. Even Cubs games, though they’d make a man suffer more than celebrate half the time. My brother used to say being a Cubs fan builds character, but I don’t know about that…\n\nAnyway, now I mostly just sit in my chair. People mention those things and I know I used to care, but I can’t seem to feel anything about them now.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way, with little interest in activities and thoughts about not waking up?\n\nPatient: It’s been… since my bowling partner died. That was about a year and a month ago now, I think.\n\nFunny, we weren’t even the best bowlers in the league—he had this way of blaming the lanes every time he missed a spare—but after he was gone, things just… went flat. That’s when I started feeling this way, more or less. A little over a year.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in activities that you used to enjoy, or is it more that you're just not able to do them like you used to?\n\nPatient: It’s more that I don’t care anymore, I think. I mean, I could probably still sit at a bridge table if someone dragged me there… though I’d forget half the bidding, I suppose. My wife used to say I always overbid anyway.\n\nBut no, it’s not just that I can’t do them. The garden’s still there, the church still does the cooking days, and the Cubs are still breaking hearts like they always do. I used to look forward to all that. Now I just sit in my chair and… I can’t make myself care about any of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you had periods where these feelings of not caring and thoughts of not waking up went away, only to come back?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think it really went away. It’s been pretty steady since my bowling partner died—about a year and a month now.\n\nWe used to bowl on Thursdays, and he’d always complain about the oil on the lanes like it was a conspiracy… but after he passed, it just all sort of settled over me. I can’t really point to a stretch where I felt like myself again and then it came back. It’s just been there.\n\nDoctor: Are these feelings of not caring and thoughts of not waking up something you experience for most of the day?\n\nPatient: Most of the day, yes… I’d say so. I mean, I sit in that chair and the day just sort of goes by.\n\nI used to look forward to things—bridge, puttering in the garden, helping with the church cooking, even going to Cubs games, though Lord knows they test a man’s patience. Now it’s like… they’re all still there, but I can’t make myself care enough to get up for them. It’s just there with me most of the time.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling this way, with little interest in things and thoughts about not waking up, for most of every day over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yes… most every day, I suppose. Not just the last two weeks. It’s been going on since my bowling partner died—about a year and a month now.\n\nFunny thing, he used to keep score even when the machine did it, said he didn’t trust computers… but after he was gone, everything just kind of… stayed gray. So yes, over the past two weeks too.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you've stopped doing because you no longer find pleasure in them?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I’ve stopped most of it, I suppose. Bridge, for one. I used to like sitting there with the cards, trying to remember who bid what—my wife used to say I played too slow, but then she said that about how I drove too.\n\nAnd the garden… I used to fuss over the tomatoes like they were children. Church cooking too, and going to Cubs games. I used to look forward to all that. Now I mostly just sit in my chair, and I can’t make myself care enough to go. It’s all still there, but it doesn’t feel like anything to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling this way for more than two years?\n\nPatient: No… not more than two years. It feels long, but it’s been since my bowling partner died—about a year and a month ago now.\n\nWe used to bowl Tuesdays, unless the lanes were taken for one of those league things, and he had this old towel he’d wipe the ball with every time like it was some fancy ritual… but no, not two years. About thirteen months.\n\nDoctor: Has this feeling of not caring and having thoughts of not waking up been consistent for the past two years or more?\n\nPatient: No… not two years. I know I keep circling around it, but it started after my bowling partner died—about a year and a month ago now. We used to have our little routine at the lanes, and after he was gone, I just… stopped caring about things the same way. So no, not two years or more.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… less than two years. It started after my bowling partner died, about a year and a month ago. Funny, I still think about the sound of the pins sometimes, that hollow crack, and how he’d always complain the shoes never fit right… but yes, about thirteen months, not two years.

I don’t know. Winters used to be different, you know, when the house was busier and the furnace made that clanking noise all night… but now I just keep thinking maybe I’ve had enough.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts of actually harming yourself or making an attempt to end your life?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I don’t think of it like making some big attempt, if that’s what you mean. It’s more that I keep thinking maybe if I just didn’t wake up, that would be all right. My wife and the kids—well, they’ve had enough worry because of me, and I get to thinking they’d be better off without me here.\n\nAnd I’ve said it, too… that I don’t need to see another winter. I know that sounds awful. Winters used to be something, with the house busy and all that, but now it just feels like… maybe enough.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been having these thoughts about not waking up and feeling like your family would be better off without you?\n\nPatient: It started after my bowling partner died… that was, let me think, about a year and a month ago now. We used to go every week, same lane if we could get it—he had this way of fussing with the score even when it didn’t matter.\n\nAfter he was gone, that’s when those thoughts started creeping in. So… a little over a year, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself enjoying things you used to like, or has that changed for you over the past year?\n\nPatient: It’s changed, yes. I used to look forward to bridge nights, and I had my garden out back—tomatoes never came in right unless I fussed over them—and the church cooking, too. My wife used to say I chopped onions too slow, but I liked being useful.\n\nAnd Cubs games… I’d get excited about going, even if they lost, which they usually found a way to do. Now I mostly just sit in my chair. People mention those things and I know I ought to care, but I can’t seem to make myself.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure or enjoyment in?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I try to think of something… bridge, maybe, or the garden. I used to spend half a day with those tomato plants, and I’d complain about it, but I liked it. Church cooking, Cubs games too—those were things I looked forward to.\n\nNow I mostly just end up in my chair. Someone will say, “Come on, you used to enjoy this,” and I know they’re right, but it’s like I can’t reach the feeling anymore. I just can’t make myself care about any of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight over the past year?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’ve kept track, honestly. My pants maybe feel a little different some days, but then laundry does that too—my wife used to say I’d blame the dryer for everything.\n\nI haven’t been weighing myself regular, so I couldn’t tell you if it’s a significant amount.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself moving or speaking more slowly than you used to?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I suppose I do. My wife says I move like I’m walking through deep snow. That’s how she puts it. I’ll be trying to get dressed and somehow it takes forever, like my arms don’t want to go where they’re supposed to.\n\nEven just crossing the kitchen… and it’s not a big kitchen, never was, though we did talk once about knocking out a wall—anyway, it takes me so long I sometimes just stop. I give up before I’ve done much of anything. Speaking too, maybe. I know I’m slower than I used to be.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty or blame yourself for things that have happened?\n\nPatient: Yes… all the time, really. I go back over things I said to him, things I didn’t say, the way I handled things with the family. It just keeps showing me every place I failed. And after a while it feels like… well, like I’m no good to anybody. Like whatever punishment is coming, I probably earned it.\n\nDoctor: Are there any small pleasures in life that you still look forward to or enjoy?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I used to have little things… bridge on Thursdays, if the fellows weren’t arguing too much about bidding, and the garden—tomatoes never came in right unless I fussed over them. Church cooking too, though I mostly chopped onions and got in the way. And Cubs games, of course, even when they were breaking your heart.\n\nBut now I just… sit in my chair. I think about doing those things, or someone mentions them, and I can’t make myself care. It’s like they belong to somebody else.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure or enjoyment in, no matter how small they may seem?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. I used to look forward to bridge, and fussing around in the garden, and the church cooking days—though half the time I was just peeling potatoes or chopping onions, you know, nothing important. And going to Cubs games, or even just watching them, that used to mean something.\n\nNow I mostly just sit in my chair. People ask if I want to do those things and I… I can’t seem to care. It all feels far away, like it belongs to someone else.\n\nDoctor: Are there any small things that you still find give you some happiness or pleasure?\n\nPatient: No… not really. I keep trying to think of one, but nothing comes to mind.\n\nI used to look forward to things, you know—bridge, puttering in the garden, helping with the church cooking. Even Cubs games, though they’d make a man suffer more than celebrate half the time. My brother used to say being a Cubs fan builds character, but I don’t know about that…\n\nAnyway, now I mostly just sit in my chair. People mention those things and I know I used to care, but I can’t seem to feel anything about them now.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way, with little interest in activities and thoughts about not waking up?\n\nPatient: It’s been… since my bowling partner died. That was about a year and a month ago now, I think.\n\nFunny, we weren’t even the best bowlers in the league—he had this way of blaming the lanes every time he missed a spare—but after he was gone, things just… went flat. That’s when I started feeling this way, more or less. A little over a year.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in activities that you used to enjoy, or is it more that you're just not able to do them like you used to?\n\nPatient: It’s more that I don’t care anymore, I think. I mean, I could probably still sit at a bridge table if someone dragged me there… though I’d forget half the bidding, I suppose. My wife used to say I always overbid anyway.\n\nBut no, it’s not just that I can’t do them. The garden’s still there, the church still does the cooking days, and the Cubs are still breaking hearts like they always do. I used to look forward to all that. Now I just sit in my chair and… I can’t make myself care about any of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you had periods where these feelings of not caring and thoughts of not waking up went away, only to come back?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think it really went away. It’s been pretty steady since my bowling partner died—about a year and a month now.\n\nWe used to bowl on Thursdays, and he’d always complain about the oil on the lanes like it was a conspiracy… but after he passed, it just all sort of settled over me. I can’t really point to a stretch where I felt like myself again and then it came back. It’s just been there.\n\nDoctor: Are these feelings of not caring and thoughts of not waking up something you experience for most of the day?\n\nPatient: Most of the day, yes… I’d say so. So yes, over the past two weeks too.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you've stopped doing because you no longer find pleasure in them?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I’ve stopped most of it, I suppose.
